companies/mistral-ai/mistral-medium-3.5/serving.md

Mistral Medium 3.5: serving on 8×B300

Status: unbenchmarked local recommendations. The upstream recipes are evidence of documented support, not a benchmark of your node.

Engine and topology

Use vLLM first because the producer explicitly recommends it and documents the Mistral reasoning and tool parsers. Establish the TP8 publisher baseline with the atlas’s 32K cap, then compare the TP2 probe and a TP4 experiment. Keep a fixed number of total node GPUs when comparing independent replicas against tensor sharding.

Resolve the correct tokenizer and Mistral encoding dependencies in the image. Do not copy an old nightly-install command and label it reproducible: pin the wheel or image digest and record the loaded config checksum. The card’s historical dependency minima are not a complete tested bill of materials.

The pinned publisher card also supplies a SGLang TP8 command with --tool-call-parser mistral and --reasoning-parser mistral. The official SGLang cookbook documents this exact FP8 model, the alternate checkpoint layouts and H200 validation runs. This establishes documented support; it does not certify the atlas’s B300 image or settings. The local SGLang profile remains a compatibility probe until loaded and tested, and a parser-free probe cannot establish reasoning or tool-call correctness. mistral-medium-3.5-GR

mistral-medium-3.5-CARD HW

Experiment sequence

After the TP8 correctness baseline, measure TP1 if it fits the workload, TP2, TP4 and TP8 with the same total node GPU budget. Keep KV precision fixed while comparing native FP8 weights; cover 4K/32K/128K prompts, one versus multiple images, and reasoning_effort=none versus high. Include a regression that records mscale_all_dim=0.0 and checks long-context output quality.

First capture actual GPU memory, compute capability, peer topology and software versions with python scripts/preflight.py. Then resolve immutable model metadata, inspect the rendered profile and run the target-only correctness suite. Profiles live in profiles.

Acceptance gates

A successful load is necessary but insufficient. Require valid streaming framing, finite outputs, correctly separated reasoning, valid tool calls where supported, repeatable quality tests, bounded memory at load, and correct cache/state behavior after cancellation. Only then compare latency and throughput.

For production, place one sharded model process group inside a pod that owns its whole GPU group. Do not make eight independent KServe replicas and expect them to form TP8. Start with colocated prefill and decode; adopt disaggregation only after it wins the workload comparison and state transfer is correct. KS KSLLM

Parameters are not magic constants

The profile’s context cap is an application policy. Tensor parallelism is a starting layout. Memory utilization reserves room for non-weight allocations; changing it can trade cache space against startup headroom. Batch-token and sequence caps trade prefill efficiency against memory and queueing. Speculative depth trades draft/verification cost against naturally accepted tokens. Each must be varied one at a time before interaction testing.

Remaining uncertainty

Installed-image and B300 compatibility at the pinned corrected revision; runtime memory beyond the reported weight payload; maximum safe concurrency and best TP width; no audited NVFP4 variant. SGLang support is documented upstream, but has not been run locally.

Kimi K3

Reverified 2026-10-15 (UTC). Publisher reports 2.8T total / 104B active; hybrid KDA + gated MLA; latent MoE.

Treat K3 as a large hybrid system, not as a conventional trillion-parameter Transformer with a smaller file format. The ordinary release is already a low-precision, quantization-aware-trained artifact. The NVIDIA NVFP4 release is a different mixed-format artifact with a different loading and kernel contract. kimi-k3-CARD kimi-k3-CFG

First serving candidate: SGLang initial candidate for NVIDIA mixed NVFP4; compare vLLM and SGLang for native MXFP4. This is a reasoned deployment starting point, not a measured winner on your hardware.

The mental model

A very large consulting firm keeps short working notes at most desks and periodically consults the detailed archive. A dispatcher sends each problem to a few specialists, but every specialist’s reference material still occupies the building. The draft assistant proposes the next several words; the lead consultant must check them before release.

companies/moonshot-ai/kimi-k3/architecture.md

Kimi K3: architecture

Data flow

Text / vision features → KDA or gated MLA → attention-residual mixing → dense or latent MoE → repeat → output head; optional DSpark proposes → target verifies → commit accepted state.

How the parts work together

The text backbone alternates frequent Kimi Delta Attention (KDA) updates with periodic gated multi-head latent attention (MLA). KDA carries a recurrent summary forward; the MLA layers retain access to token history through compressed attention representations. This combination makes the model neither ordinary full attention everywhere nor a constant-memory model everywhere. A long conversation still consumes token-cache memory in the MLA layers.

The feed-forward path is a latent mixture of experts. Routing selects specialists, but the specialist computation is represented in a narrower latent space rather than treating every expert as a full-width dense feed-forward network. The dense prefix, shared experts, routers, normalization, and residual machinery still perform work. The number of selected experts therefore does not by itself determine end-to-end FLOPs or latency.

The configuration also exposes block attention-residual organization. Do not substitute a plain residual-add implementation simply because tensor shapes happen to agree. Likewise, SiTU is not interchangeable with a default SwiGLU activation: the checkpoint-dependent activation and scaling must survive expert dispatch, quantization, fusion, and reduction.

Image or video processing adds a vision encoder and transport path before language inference. Its memory and CPU-transfer overhead must be budgeted separately from text tokens. A text-only benchmark does not certify multimodal throughput.

The architecture facts above are drawn from the publisher card and configuration; the workload consequences are analytical interpretation. kimi-k3-CARD kimi-k3-CFG

Configuration extraction

This is a selected, normalized projection, not a byte-for-byte copy of the complete config. Field labels have been made comparable across models. Layer indices explicitly state zero-based or one-based conventions.

Source for the extraction: kimi-k3-CFG. Headline counts, modality descriptions and advertised extended context are additionally drawn from kimi-k3-CARD. A transformers_version metadata field is not by itself proof of a minimum compatible package version.

Memory and performance consequences

The state pool is a separate concurrency limit from the MLA KV pool. Adding DCP can improve one pool without sharding the recurrent state in the same way. In a head-sharded thought experiment, 69 layers × 96 heads × 128 × 128 FP32 entries is exactly 434,110,464 bytes (414 MiB) of matrix state per sequence before convolution state, speculative snapshots, padding, and implementation-specific sharing. This is an illustrative formula, not an allocator measurement. Divide only by a parallelism dimension that the actual implementation uses to shard those heads.

Using the rounded publisher count, a hypothetical all-FP8 2.8T model needs approximately 2.8 TB (decimal) for idealized weights alone. That does not fit into the advertised 2.304 TB aggregate of eight B300 GPUs. Native MXFP4 and the mixed NVFP4 export change that conclusion, but usable KV and state capacity must still be established after loading and CUDA graph capture.

The numeric examples are calculations from stated dimensions and assumptions, not measured allocations. Memory mathematics explains the formulas and their limits. kimi-k3-CARD kimi-k3-CFG HW

What this report does not establish

Exact B300 SXM6-AC OEM topology; all-FP8 artifact availability; exact installed-image ancestry of mixed-format fixes; maximum safe long-context concurrency; native-versus-NVFP4 quality on your tools and languages; actual DSpark acceptance on your workload.

The public artifacts do not disclose every training-data, optimization or implementation detail. This report does not fill missing details with assumptions or present a complete formal reconstruction of private training.

companies/moonshot-ai/kimi-k3/dspark.md

Kimi K3: DSpark integration and B300 state budget

What the draft model actually does

The inspected vLLM K3DSparkModel uses a dense MLA draft path rather than the target's full latent MoE. Its context projection consumes selected target-layer features, while the target embedding can be aliased after draft loading. The implementation imports separate confidence and Markov heads. Draft-layer attention uses non-causal multi-token decoding; selected row-parallel output reductions are fused with subsequent RMS normalization. These observations concern the inspected implementation, not an assertion that every DSpark checkpoint has the same dimensions.

Read the pinned implementation and the engine recipe. Target features, vocabulary mapping, checkpoint revisions, cache format and engine implementation must agree. A different drafter name is not a drop-in optimization.

Analogy: the main author supplies a sketch of the argument to a faster assistant. The assistant proposes several words, but the author checks them together. Speed depends on useful proposals and cheap checking, not merely on the assistant being small.

The two named drafts are different artifacts

DraftImmutable revisionConfiguration distinction
Inferact/Kimi-K3-DSparkcf6b8244620e7ea4b0651d214f28e89eac75bed6Five dense MLA layers; target feature IDs [2, 23, 47, 71, 89]
RadixArk/Kimi-K3-DSpark3c5bac301d9cf392706189d82ed947feca6c2f0fFive full-attention layers, 64 query heads and 16 KV heads; target feature IDs [7, 23, 51, 67, 83]

These are the serialized feature IDs, not a promise of interchangeable indexing semantics across engines. Token IDs, attention layouts and positional configurations also differ. The Inferact card documents vLLM; the RadixArk card documents SGLang. Pin the appropriate draft independently of the target. Inferact config, RadixArk config.

Both cards publish acceptance measurements on named datasets, but those figures use their own sampling and aggregation definitions. They do not validate the SGLang cookbook's simulated-acceptance B300 speed rows. Inferact's peak-speed description pairs “4 × GB300” with tensor-parallel-size=16 without clarifying whether the four count is nodes; that topology needs clarification before reuse. This atlas does not use the peak number to rank engines. Inferact card, RadixArk card.

Why DCP8 does not divide all memory by eight

The SGLang cookbook describes a split between target MLA cache, recurrent KDA state and drafter cache. In its published sizing rule, target MLA token cache is divided by DCP, while the DSpark draft token cache remains replicated. Thus a small replicated cache can become significant after the larger target cache is sharded.

state_to_KV_pool_ratio =
    (ordinary_state_slots + intermediate_verify_slots) * state_slot_bytes
    / (average_input_plus_output_length
       * (target_MLA_bytes_per_token / DCP + draft_bytes_per_token))

The cookbook defines five ordinary slots for extra_buffer, four for extra_buffer_lazy, three for no_buffer, and one when radix caching is disabled, with additional scheduler-dependent adjustments. DSpark verification normally introduces block-size-plus-one intermediate states. ReplaySSM changes that allocation contract by using replay rings. These are version-specific engine semantics, not architecture constants. Read actual admission and cache-capacity logs after every change. SGLang cookbook

Current recipe versus a generic latest image

At retrieval, the SGLang cookbook specifically points the NVIDIA NVFP4 export at lmsysorg/sglang:dev-dev-kimi-k3-nvfp4. That is a mutable development tag, not a verified immutable image in this atlas. The compatibility merge is an ancestor of v0.5.21, but source inclusion does not establish the complete tested image/dependency stack. The cookbook and older card still name a development image; preserve that distinction when reconciling the documentation. Resolve the intended image to a digest and validate its actual code/dependencies before using the included candidates.

Published B300 DSpark rows use SGLANG_SIMULATE_ACC_LEN=4.5, making them controlled acceptance experiments rather than measured application acceptance. The local launcher rejects simulated-acceptance environment variables. The source's B300 measurement setup and its older launch-era text also have different dates: retain the exact measurement version and do not relabel it as current-nightly performance. SGLang cookbook

Begin with the target-only profile, then run the explicit DSpark profile with a pinned drafter and real prompts. Match thinking effort, preserve the model's expected multi-turn message fields, and test rejection rollback, cancellation, prefix hits and eviction. The producer requires the complete returned assistant message, including reasoning and tool fields, to be preserved for subsequent turns. Kimi model card

Kimi K3: precision and provenance

Interpretation

Moonshot describes MXFP4/MXFP8 quantization-aware training. The serialized config specifies MXFP4 groups of 32 and lists excluded modules; it leaves input_activations unset. That training description does not prove that every module or serving backend runs MXFP8 activations. NVIDIA describes BF16 attention in the original checkpoint. Base card, base config, NVIDIA card.

The derivative's config declares ModelOpt MIXED_PRECISION: expert weight/activation groups of 16 and separate FP8_PB_WO attention entries. NVIDIA reports an approximately 1.6 TB download. Download size is not resident GPU memory; scales, runtime repacking, caches and temporary allocations require separate accounting. Derivative config, NVIDIA card.

Runtime evidence

SGLang PR #35077 merged on 2026-08-19 and is an ancestor of the v0.5.21 tag. Its author reports a correctness run on 8×GB300 and explicitly reports no speed measurement. Release-source inclusion is established; the exact installed wheel or image and its dependency stack have not been tested here. The current cookbook still points this export to the mutable lmsysorg/sglang:dev-dev-kimi-k3-nvfp4 image. Do not repeat the older model card's blanket statement that pip SGLang cannot load it as a current release conclusion. Compatibility PR, release ancestry, cookbook.

NVIDIA also documents a patched vLLM path on 8×B300, using its bundled runtime_patches/sitecustomize.py and the mutable vllm/vllm-openai:kimi-k3 image. This is publisher-reported compatibility, not a local reproduction. The three linked upstream PRs — mixed dispatch #50617, attention support #52406 and SiTU scaling #52405 — remain open at this audit. The included generic vLLM NVFP4 profile therefore stays blocked pending an audited compatible image; the publisher's patched route is a separate option. NVIDIA instructions, #50617, #52406, #52405.

Deployment rule

Pin the target and tokenizer/processor revisions, quantization configuration, runtime commit, compatibility patch where used, and image digest together. Root BF16 metadata can coexist with quantized weights. Check serialized tensor types, scales, grouping, excluded modules, activation precision and runtime repacking; casting this checkpoint to BF16 does not reconstruct an original BF16 reference.

Compare target-only quality before changing KV precision or enabling speculation. The precision primer explains the formats; the validation ledger defines the local evidence still needed.

License provenance

The pinned base repository contains the custom Kimi K3 License. The NVIDIA card names the NVIDIA Open Model Agreement and separately links Kimi terms; its checked-in LICENSE is the Kimi K3 text. Preserve both provenance references rather than labeling the derivative solely with the base license. No model weights are bundled here. Base LICENSE, derivative LICENSE, derivative terms identification.

companies/moonshot-ai/kimi-k3/serving.md

Kimi K3: serving on 8×B300

Status: unbenchmarked local recommendations. The upstream recipes are evidence of documented support, not a benchmark of your node.

Engine and topology

Start with a single eight-GPU target. For native K3, compare SGLang TP8 at 64K context against vLLM’s model-specific recipe. For the NVIDIA derivative, prefer the SGLang mixed-format path as the initial compatibility candidate, with the FlashInfer TRT-LLM MoE runner required by its recipe. Its published DCP8 setup differs from the plain TP8 target-only profile included here. The cookbook disables symmetric memory under DCP; choose a documented topology before combining flags.

Establish target-only correctness first. Then attach the engine-specific drafter: Inferact’s MLA-native DSpark is documented for vLLM, while the RadixArk drafter is documented in the SGLang path. Tokenizer, hidden-state feature selection, target revision, drafter revision, KV precision, and speculative state rollback are one compatibility unit.

The repository includes separate target-only and DSpark profiles. It intentionally does not enable simulated acceptance. The SGLang cookbook marks some B300 rows as verified and other layouts as extrapolations; its simulated-acceptance results are capacity/performance experiments, not observed application acceptance. The published Inferact peak figure is not a measurement of your eight-B300 node, and its written hardware count and TP degree need clarification; see the DSpark audit.

kimi-k3-VR kimi-k3-GR kimi-k3-CARD HW

NVIDIA also publishes a vLLM route using a bundled runtime patch. Its existence prevents a blanket claim that this derivative is unsupported by vLLM; it does not validate the atlas’s generic vLLM profile. The precision report records the patch and still-open upstream PRs.

Experiment sequence

Sweep target-only versus 3/5/7-token drafting on real coding and multi-turn agent prompts. Measure accepted tokens, total emitted tokens, draft overhead, target verification cost, state-pool ceiling, TTFT and p95 token latency. Compare context 16K/64K/128K before attempting 1M. Test cancellation and resumption with non-empty speculative state.

First capture actual GPU memory, compute capability, peer topology and software versions with python scripts/preflight.py. Then verify local metadata against the immutable revisions in model.json, inspect the rendered profile and run the target-only correctness suite. Profiles live in profiles.

Acceptance gates

A successful load is necessary but insufficient. Require valid streaming framing, finite outputs, correctly separated reasoning, valid tool calls where supported, repeatable quality tests, bounded memory at load, and correct cache/state behavior after cancellation. Only then compare latency and throughput.

For production, place one sharded model process group inside a pod that owns its whole GPU group. Do not make eight independent KServe replicas and expect them to form TP8. Start with colocated prefill and decode; adopt disaggregation only after it wins the workload comparison and state transfer is correct. KS KSLLM

Parameters are not magic constants

The profile’s context cap is an application policy. Tensor parallelism is a starting layout. Memory utilization reserves room for non-weight allocations; changing it can trade cache space against startup headroom. Batch-token and sequence caps trade prefill efficiency against memory and queueing. Speculative depth trades draft/verification cost against naturally accepted tokens. Each must be varied one at a time before interaction testing.

Remaining uncertainty

Exact B300 SXM6-AC OEM topology; all-FP8 artifact availability; exact installed-image ancestry of mixed-format fixes; maximum safe long-context concurrency; native-versus-NVFP4 quality on your tools and languages; actual DSpark acceptance on your workload.

companies/z-ai/glm-5.3/architecture.md

GLM 5.3: architecture

Data flow

Tokens → dense prefix → MLA latent cache + DSA index selection → routed/shared experts → repeat → output; optional MTP → target verification.

How the parts work together

The configuration describes MLA with a separate sparse-attention indexer. The model first constructs representations that make token-history storage cheaper than retaining a separate full key/value vector for every query head. The indexer then selects a limited subset of history for the more expensive attention operation. Compression of what is stored and sparsification of what is read are distinct optimizations.

A dense prefix is followed by routed MoE layers. Shared experts provide a common feed-forward contribution while selected routed experts specialize the token transformation. The router uses its own numerical precision and score function; expert quantization is not permission to quantize router decisions without validation.

The index-selection frequency exposes reuse across layers. Reusing index selections can avoid repeated work, but correctness depends on the implementation’s layer boundaries and index cache. It is not equivalent to asking every layer to select a fresh arbitrary top-k set.

The multi-token-prediction (MTP) extension can propose multiple steps for speculative serving. It does not convert the main model into a dense small model. Generation remains a target-model process with draft verification and cache bookkeeping.

The architecture facts above are drawn from the publisher card and configuration; the workload consequences are analytical interpretation. glm-5.3-CARD glm-5.3-CFG

Configuration extraction

This is a selected, normalized projection, not a byte-for-byte copy of the complete config. Field labels have been made comparable across models. Layer indices explicitly state zero-based or one-based conventions.

Memory and performance consequences

The parameter scopes can now be reconciled. The pinned official FP8 and BF16 Hub metadata each report 753,329,940,480 parameters. The BF16 index maps 791 tensors to the extra layer model.layers.78; HTTP range reads of their five safetensors headers give 9,952,920,576 parameters. Subtracting that MTP layer gives 743,377,019,904 main-model parameters, consistent with the recipe's approximate 743B headline. The 39B active figure remains a recipe headline, not an independently counted active path. This reconciles named tensors in the stored artifacts; it does not independently verify unique training parameters, deduplicate tied/shared weights or constitute a complete tensor census. glm-5.3-META glm-5.3-BF16-INDEX Header retrievals

The FP8 index reports 755,617,140,416 serialized tensor bytes (755.62 decimal GB); the BF16 index reports 1,506,659,919,872 bytes (1,506.66 GB). These include the stored checkpoint's precision mix and are not CUDA load measurements. Both payloads are below the reference node's advertised 8 × 288 GB aggregate capacity, but per-rank allocation, KV/indexer caches, graph capture, temporary buffers and desired concurrency determine whether a launch works. A lower scheduling limit alone does not prove that one maximum-length request fits. glm-5.3-INDEX glm-5.3-BF16-INDEX

The numeric examples are calculations from stated dimensions and assumptions, not measured allocations. Memory mathematics explains the formulas and their limits. glm-5.3-CARD glm-5.3-CFG HW

What this report does not establish

Actual per-rank load and cache capacity on this node; exporter-specific quality under the application prompts; inclusion of required fixes in the selected image; and the workload crossover between vLLM and SGLang remain unmeasured. The 743B/753B accounting is now reconciled from pinned metadata.

The public artifacts do not disclose every training-data, optimization or implementation detail. This report does not fill missing details with assumptions or present a complete formal reconstruction of private training.

GLM 5.3: precision and provenance

All four artifacts below were checked at immutable revisions on 2026-10-15. A reviewed card/config is evidence of the serialization contract, not proof of successful GPU execution.

VariantPinned artifactPrecision contract from configurationIndex tensor bytes
fp8zai-org/GLM-5.3Block FP8 E4M3, 128×128 weight blocks, dynamic activations, explicit exclusions755,617,140,416
bf16zai-org/GLM-5.3-BF16BF16 reference with a small FP32 component; no quantization config1,506,659,919,872
nvfp4RedHatAI/GLM-5.3-NVFP4compressed-tensors NVFP4 W4A4 on main routed-expert linears, group size 16; MTP and other modules excluded464,824,469,712
nvfp4-inferactInferact/GLM-5.3-NVFP4ModelOpt NVFP4 on main routed experts, group size 16; MTP excluded; exporter-specific calibration metadata464,822,832,448

The byte figures are each index's metadata.total_size, not measured HBM allocations or complete repository download sizes. Full revisions and index links are in sources and model.json. The near-equal RedHat and Inferact payload sizes do not establish equal calibration or quality. RedHat explicitly excludes model.layers.78 with a regex; Inferact lists its MTP expert module among the exclusions. Thus the NVFP4 scope does not include the stored MTP expert weights.

Quality evidence

RedHat reports IFEval prompt-level strict accuracy of 85.70% for NVFP4 versus 89.77% for its local FP8 baseline, a 4.07 percentage-point difference. Its evaluation uses the same harness/serving stack, FP8 KV, temperature 1.0 and 4×B200; these are exporter results, not reproduced B300 measurements. They establish that blanket lossless-quantization language is inappropriate, without isolating every source of the difference. glm-5.3-NV

Test instruction following, tool argument fidelity, retrieval and multilingual output on the application workload. Keep the reasoning effort and conversation template identical across precision variants.

Deployment rule

Pin the model, tokenizer/processor, quantization config, engine commit and container digest together. A BF16 root dtype can coexist with quantized tensors. Serialized tensor types, scales, grouping, exclusions, activation precision and loader repacking jointly determine the execution contract. Casting an FP8 checkpoint to BF16 does not recover the original BF16 weights.

Establish target-only quality before adding lower-precision KV or speculation. Precision primer explains the format distinctions.

License provenance

The pinned publisher file is titled GLM-5.3 License. Record it as a custom license and inspect its conditions for the intended use; do not infer terms from another GLM release. The RedHat card identifies the same license name. No model tensor payloads are bundled here. glm-5.3-LICENSE

companies/z-ai/glm-5.3/serving.md

GLM 5.3: serving on 8×B300

Status: unbenchmarked local recommendations. The upstream recipes are evidence of documented support, not a benchmark of your node.

Engine and topology

Use vLLM FP8 TP8 with a deliberately capped 64K application context as the first target-only comparison. Compare TP4 for NVFP4 against TP8: four-way tensor parallelism could leave room for two independent four-GPU replicas, but only if each process really fits with the desired workload and no overlapping GPU assignment.

Add the MTP profile after the non-speculative baseline passes. The recipe’s five speculative tokens are a documented candidate, not a measurement on this node. In the current SGLang cookbook, the reasoning parser resolves to glm45 and the tool-call parser to glm47; the latter parses the newer <tool_call>/<arg_key>/<arg_value> format. Use those distinct roles when checking the SGLang profile.

The official vLLM recipe contains inconsistent historical minimum-version labels. The lock file records the current release and inspected main separately rather than pretending that the recipe proves one exact minimum supported version.

glm-5.3-VR glm-5.3-GR glm-5.3-CARD HW

Experiment sequence

Compare FP8 TP8; NVFP4 TP4; NVFP4 TP8; two isolated TP4 replicas; and MTP off versus 1/3/5 proposals. Report total node goodput, not only one replica’s tokens/second. Verify the same reasoning effort and retained conversation fields. The pinned publisher template defaults to reasoning_effort=max and clear_thinking=false; the publisher recommends explicitly enabling clear_thinking for chat. Treat those choices as workload settings, not performance-free toggles.

First capture actual GPU memory, compute capability, peer topology and software versions with python scripts/preflight.py. Then resolve immutable model metadata, inspect the rendered profile and run the target-only correctness suite. Profiles live in profiles.

The included FP8 vLLM profile explicitly selects FP8 KV, while the NVFP4 profile leaves KV selection to the engine. Record the effective cache dtype and align it through a validated setting before attributing differences to weight precision; otherwise treat cache precision as a separate experimental variable.

Acceptance gates

A successful load is necessary but insufficient. Require valid streaming framing, finite outputs, correctly separated reasoning, valid tool calls where supported, repeatable quality tests, bounded memory at load, and correct cache/state behavior after cancellation. Only then compare latency and throughput.

For production, place one sharded model process group inside a pod that owns its whole GPU group. Do not make eight independent KServe replicas and expect them to form TP8. Start with colocated prefill and decode; adopt disaggregation only after it wins the workload comparison and state transfer is correct. KS KSLLM

Parameters are not magic constants

The profile’s context cap is an application policy. Tensor parallelism is a starting layout. Memory utilization reserves room for non-weight allocations; changing it can trade cache space against startup headroom. Batch-token and sequence caps trade prefill efficiency against memory and queueing. Speculative depth trades draft/verification cost against naturally accepted tokens. Each must be varied one at a time before interaction testing.

Remaining uncertainty

Actual per-rank load and cache capacity on this node; exporter-specific quality under the application prompts; inclusion of required fixes in the selected image; and the workload crossover between vLLM and SGLang remain unmeasured. The 743B/753B accounting is now reconciled. See architecture for the pinned metadata calculation.

companies/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash/architecture.md

GLM 5.3 Flash: architecture

Data flow

Vision/text → hybrid attention and MoE blocks with four residual streams → text head. KDA layers maintain recurrent state; sparse MLA layers read selected token history. MTP proposes tokens, and the serving engine must verify them with the target and commit the corresponding accepted state.

How the parts work together

Most text layers use KDA, while periodic NoPE sparse-MLA layers retrieve selected history. NoPE means that those attention projections do not use the ordinary rotary-position component; it does not mean that the whole model has no way to process sequence structure. The attention indexer includes pooled compression and a tail-selection mechanism.

Manifold-constrained hyper-connections maintain multiple residual streams and constrain how information mixes between them. The repeated Sinkhorn normalization configured for these connections is a specific model operation, not a generic replacement for the RMSNorm layer. Fusing residual communication must preserve that transformation.

The MoE has many more resident experts than each token activates. Its shared path, dense prefix and router remain part of the total compute and memory budget. A vision stack maps images and video frames into the text network’s representation; limiting image count is an admission-control decision as well as a preprocessing decision.

The native MTP layer is served through different APIs in the two engines. SGLang’s current recipe uses the EAGLE algorithm spelling for this native MTP path. That does not mean the model is using an unrelated external EAGLE checkpoint.

The architecture facts above are drawn from the publisher card and configuration; the workload consequences are analytical interpretation. glm-5.3-flash-CARD glm-5.3-flash-CFG

Configuration extraction

This is a selected, normalized projection, not a byte-for-byte copy of the complete config. Field labels have been made comparable across models. Layer indices explicitly state zero-based or one-based conventions.

Source for the extraction, rechecked on 2026-10-15 at 46a050bec13002ad29ee795c16e61cf4fa3c4a9c: glm-5.3-flash-CFG. Headline counts, modality descriptions and advertised extended context are additionally drawn from glm-5.3-flash-CARD. A transformers_version metadata field is not by itself proof of a minimum compatible package version.

Memory and performance consequences

The publisher rounds the model to 320B total / 18B active. Pinned Hub metadata for both official precision variants reports 321,323,031,390 parameters; the catalog uses that checkpoint total for weight-only arithmetic. The publisher's active-parameter count has not been independently reconstructed. Packed NVFP4 arrays and their scale tensors cannot be counted as if every stored scalar were an original model parameter. glm-5.3-flash-META

The official FP8 index reports 328,326,771,576 serialized tensor bytes (328.33 decimal GB); BF16 reports 642,646,653,816 bytes (642.65 GB). Runtime memory also includes caches, recurrent state, temporary buffers and any loader repacking. glm-5.3-flash-INDEX glm-5.3-flash-BF16-INDEX

Budget recurrent KDA state and sparse token cache independently. SGLang’s cookbook explicitly warns that its default Mamba-to-full-memory ratio is generic. Too little state capacity caps running requests; too much starves token history. Derive a candidate split from real request lengths and boot-log pool sizes instead of blindly maximizing the ratio.

For a simple state-size illustration, 34 × 64 × 128 × 128 × 4 bytes is 142,606,336 bytes = 136 MiB per sequence for the FP32 state matrices alone, excluding convolution state, MTP state and implementation overhead. This is not the final CUDA allocation and cannot be divided by EP or DCP unless the source path shards that state accordingly.

The numeric examples are calculations from stated dimensions and assumptions, not measured allocations. Memory mathematics explains the formulas and their limits. glm-5.3-flash-CARD glm-5.3-flash-CFG HW

What this report does not establish

The RedHat quantization configuration is now inspected at an immutable revision: main routed experts use NVFP4 and MTP routed experts use FP8. Other exporters require their own configuration audit. Quality, loader memory, the best KDA/KV split and the disaggregation crossover still need local measurements.

The public artifacts do not disclose every training-data, optimization or implementation detail. This report does not fill missing details with assumptions or present a complete formal reconstruction of private training.

companies/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash/precision.md

GLM 5.3 Flash: precision and provenance

The official FP8 and BF16 variants and the exact RedHat derivative were checked at immutable revisions on 2026-10-15.

VariantPinned artifactPrecision contract from configurationIndex tensor bytes
fp8zai-org/GLM-5.3-FlashBlock FP8 E4M3, 128×128 weight blocks, dynamic activations and explicit exclusions328,326,771,576
bf16zai-org/GLM-5.3-Flash-BF16BF16 reference with a small FP32 component; no quantization config642,646,653,816
nvfp4RedHatAI/GLM-5.3-Flash-NVFP4compressed-tensors: main routed experts NVFP4 W4A4; MTP routed experts FP8 W8A8; other modules excluded197,816,133,372

The figures come from metadata.total_size in each pinned index, linked in sources. They are serialized tensor bytes, not measured GPU allocations. RedHat's export is about 39.75% smaller by this metric than the official FP8 artifact; a four-bit label does not imply a 50% reduction for the whole mixed checkpoint.

Exact RedHat contract

The NVFP4 group targets the routed-expert projection weights and activations in zero-based main layers 3–44, with group size 16. A second group targets MTP layer 45's routed experts using 8-bit floating-point weights in 128×128 blocks and dynamic 8-bit activations. The remaining modules are excluded from these quantization groups. This narrows the card's statement that MTP is kept in FP8 to the scope directly visible in the config. glm-5.3-flash-NV-CFG

RedHat publishes selected reasoning benchmark scores but no paired FP8 table in this pinned card. Those scores do not establish lossless conversion, multimodal quality or B300 performance. NVIDIA and RadixArk exports named by the SGLang cookbook require their own config and calibration checks. glm-5.3-flash-NV

KV and deployment contract

SGLang's CUDA recipe pairs Blackwell FP8 KV with TRT-LLM DSA, or BF16 KV with TileLang DSA. Change the dtype and both DSA backends together; the cookbook says CUDA TileLang DSA plus FP8 KV is not a valid pairing for this model. Verify the exact image because this is a documented path, not a local execution result. glm-5.3-flash-GR

Pin the model, tokenizer/processor, config and engine/container revisions together. Establish target-only quality before comparing quantized KV or speculation. A root BF16 dtype does not mean every stored tensor is BF16, and casting quantized weights does not recover the original reference. See the precision primer.

License provenance

The official FP8 and BF16 artifacts contain the MIT license; the RedHat card also declares MIT. These observations apply to the inspected revisions. Check any additional exporter or bundled-code terms before redistribution. No model tensor payloads are bundled here. glm-5.3-flash-LICENSE

companies/z-ai/glm-5.3-flash/serving.md

GLM 5.3 Flash: serving on 8×B300

Status: unbenchmarked local recommendations. The upstream recipes are evidence of documented support, not a benchmark of your node.

Engine and topology

Use one TP4 target-only process as a starting candidate, then compare a second independent TP4 replica against a TP8 process. SGLang’s Blackwell cookbook selects FlashInfer TRT-LLM MoE; vLLM provides an explicit TP4 FP8 recipe and an optional TP4-prefill plus TP4-decode layout.

First determine whether prefill interference warrants prefill/decode separation. The vLLM recipe requires matching KDA convolution-state layout, KV layout and speculative depth on both sides. A KV-only copy that omits recurrent state is not a correct migration. SGLang documents its PD route as a preview with dummy-weight mechanical checks only; it lacks real-weight load/accuracy qualification, and its current documented PD path does not start with speculative decoding. Keep speculation off and both roles at the same TP size for that separate probe.

The vLLM Flash recipe still contains historical integration/version guidance and differing FlashInfer minimums (0.6.17 in prerequisites versus 0.6.18 in troubleshooting). Resolve those requirements against the selected image; the recipe alone does not certify every release or nightly.

For structured responses, the current SGLang recipe resolves the reasoning parser to glm45 and the tool-call parser to glm47; vLLM's current recipe uses glm47 for both. Verify the returned fields on the pinned image.

Use natural MTP acceptance. Test the cookbook’s five-step, top-k-one configuration against target-only operation at high load. A speculative low-latency win can become a throughput loss when verification competes with a large batch.

glm-5.3-flash-VR glm-5.3-flash-GR glm-5.3-flash-CARD HW

Experiment sequence

Compare target-only operation (omit speculation flags) with 1/3/5 draft steps; TP4 versus TP8; independent replicas versus 4+4 disaggregation; the supported KV/backend pairs (FP8 + TRT-LLM DSA versus BF16 + TileLang DSA in SGLang CUDA); controlled KDA pool sizes. Add multi-image and long multi-turn tests with cancellation, context-cache reuse and state rollback.

First capture actual GPU memory, compute capability, peer topology and software versions with python scripts/preflight.py. Then resolve immutable model metadata, inspect the rendered profile and run the target-only correctness suite. Profiles live in profiles.

Acceptance gates

A successful load is necessary but insufficient. Require valid streaming framing, finite outputs, correctly separated reasoning, valid tool calls where supported, repeatable quality tests, bounded memory at load, and correct cache/state behavior after cancellation. Only then compare latency and throughput.

For production, place one sharded model process group inside a pod that owns its whole GPU group. Do not make eight independent KServe replicas and expect them to form TP8. Start with colocated prefill and decode; adopt disaggregation only after it wins the workload comparison and state transfer is correct. KS KSLLM

Parameters are not magic constants

The profile’s context cap is an application policy. Tensor parallelism is a starting layout. Memory utilization reserves room for non-weight allocations; changing it can trade cache space against startup headroom. Batch-token and sequence caps trade prefill efficiency against memory and queueing. Speculative depth trades draft/verification cost against naturally accepted tokens. Each must be varied one at a time before interaction testing.

Remaining uncertainty

The RedHat quantization configuration is now inspected at an immutable revision: main routed experts use NVFP4 and MTP routed experts use FP8. Other exporters require their own configuration audit. Quality, loader memory, the best KDA/KV split and the disaggregation crossover still need local measurements.

deploy/kserve/README.md

Deployment rendering boundary

Read architecture first. The renderer emits a custom-container InferenceService in Standard mode. It does not apply, install controllers, create PVCs, label nodes or download models. It is not a fabricated validated LLMInferenceService disaggregation deployment.

Provide a real digest, prepopulated read-only model PVC, measured CPU/host-memory requests and a node label actually present in the cluster. The default superstorm.ai/gpu-class=B300 is a label you must deliberately assign after inventory, not an automatic NVIDIA label. A single pod allocates the entire profile's GPU group on one node. Eight TP1 replicas require eight separate allocations/routing; this example keeps replicas at one.

The initial 8Gi shared-memory size and 30-minute startup allowance are editable safety candidates, not measured needs or a promise about cold-start time. Shared memory consumes the pod's host-memory budget. Startup/readiness health paths and graceful termination must be exercised against the actual image and KServe version. GPU topology and CPU locality require cluster scheduling policies beyond a label.

The renderer checks the SHA-256 digest suffix and basic image-reference structure but does not contact the registry to prove the image exists. It explicitly disables automatic service-account token mounting, a field supported by the KServe 0.21.0 CRD. It adds no credential mounts or external-service calls. Its local tests validate structure and guards, not admission against your installed CRDs. Before deploying, use kubectl apply --dry-run=server, inspect the rendered pod and confirm offline checkpoint/drafter availability, parser correctness, runtime cache paths and resource headroom. Require gateway authentication before external exposure.

evidence/ENGINE-UPDATE-2026-10-16.md

Engine research and experiment expansion · October 16, 2026

The date in this title is Europe/Paris. Source receipts use UTC and were collected on October 15. This follow-up starts from atlas commit 5dfbab0, after the repository audit. The user requested more explanation and experiments informed by stable, recent main and nightly vLLM/SGLang/KServe changes.

Delivered scope

Three Astra max researchers expanded the framework source reviews and wrote 30 engine/platform experiment cards: vLLM V01–V10, SGLang G01–G12 and KServe K01–K08. The coordinator added the shared X01–X09 roadmap, manual run-record template, refreshed nightly synthesis, navigation and integrated evidence.

The cards state hypotheses, prerequisites, controlled changes, measurements, quality checks and rejection criteria. The roadmap covers all eight atlas families and preserves their exact artifact identities, context limits and existing blocked/probe statuses. Existing launch profiles and their historical source-inspection commits were not upgraded or promoted to tested status.

Findings that change experiment design

FindingPractical consequence
Release, inspected main and published nightly identify different objects; the vLLM channel moved during this researchKeep each dated observation. Confirm source inclusion and actual artifact/dependency identity before using a feature.
Release branching can exclude a change merged before publicationInspect the exact release tree or ancestry. SGLang Qwen graph helpers and KServe's SGLang template provide concrete examples.
The merged vLLM cuBLAS initialization differs from the PR descriptionInterpret the memory baseline from the merged code, then measure load/restart behavior.
vLLM's new MegaMoE NVFP4 row has no model caller in the inspected patch; draft-head and GLM metadata changes have explicit exclusionsTreat these as scoped research candidates. Do not invent support flags or transfer gains to unrelated methods/models.
SGLang int8 cached recurrent state is already in stable source; active state and cached state are different treatmentsIsolate capacity, restoration cost and quality after repeated cache hits. Do not advertise an existing capability as a new nightly improvement.
KServe stable includes a newer routing preset file but its default router version selects the older pathInspect rendered scheduler configuration, not file presence alone.
KServe master adds a scoped SGLang template and changes scaling/rollout behaviorSeparate the unreleased single-node template from advanced P/D integration, and qualify migration and rollback independently.

The framework reviews provide the primary URLs and pinned functions for these findings. No publisher microbenchmark ratio was promoted to a local B300 performance claim.

sources.json and SOURCES.md contain 126 fresh source records, bringing the register to 261 entries. Each new captured response retains its retrieval time, actual endpoint and hash. Local checks do not validate the experiment template's semantic completeness or prove runtime compatibility.

evidence/README.md

Evidence register and audit trail

Read LIMITATIONS before using this atlas for a purchasing or production decision.

LabelWhat it meansWhat it does not mean
Publisher/configurationA fact read in the named primary artifactIndependently reproduced training or benchmark
Source inspectedNamed lines or API metadata were read at a specific referenceWhole-repository audit or executed code
Publisher benchmarkThe author reports that setup/resultOur B300 measurement or a fair cross-engine comparison
DerivedArithmetic or engineering inference with explicit assumptionsMeasured resident memory or optimal settings
Candidate/probeA proposed local experiment with known prerequisitesProduction-ready deployment
Blocked/unknownEvidence is insufficient or compatibility is unresolvedProof the task is impossible

sources.json is the machine-readable primary-source register. SOURCES.md is the readable index. upstreams.lock.json records releases, inspected source commits, separately observed main references, PR states and exact model revisions. A model JSON contains a curated config projection and source IDs, not the complete original configuration. Later provenance must retain its actual timestamp and immutable revision.

The October 15 audit records findings and coverage. Its source-access receipts cover all 62 URLs in the initial register. A successful HTTP response establishes accessibility only; the accompanying reviewer ledgers document which content claims were checked. Response hashes identify retrieved bytes, whose complete bodies are not bundled. The historical clone-failure log does not describe the current audit environment.

The October 16 engine expansion, dated in Europe/Paris with UTC retrieval timestamps, adds stable/main/nightly research and planned experiments. Its receipts and independent reviews are kept separately from the original audit. Existing profile inspection commits remain historical identities; the lock separately records the new branch inspections and development artifact metadata.

For each production claim retain the checkpoint SHA, image digest, effective engine class and kernels, command, hardware inventory, workload hash, quality output and raw measurements. A correct loader registry entry is one condition of support, not the entire evidence chain.

No third-party weights, private repository content, tokens, proprietary PDFs or upstream font assets are bundled. Source documents remain available at their publishers. Authored prose paraphrases and explains the evidence; it is not a wholesale reproduction of model cards.

frameworks/kserve/EXPERIMENTS.md

KServe experiments after engine qualification

Status: proposed experiments; no cluster deployment or serving measurement was performed. KServe controls service deployment and routing resources; vLLM and SGLang remain the engines. These plans measure the extra request path and lifecycle behavior after the exact checkpoint, engine image and GPU topology pass the direct-engine protocol. See architecture for the freshly inspected stable/master differences.

Use the shared experiment roadmap to choose the next eligible comparison and the manual run record to retain its inputs, gates and results.

Entry conditions and evidence to retain

Start with a target-only engine configuration that loads the pinned artifact, meets the quality threshold and has a reproducible direct-service baseline. Add speculation, quantization or another engine release in separate comparisons. A platform experiment cannot repair an unqualified model/export/kernel combination.

Record the model and tokenizer revisions, engine version and digest, GPU allocation, CPU/memory resources, node/network inventory, workload hashes, cache state and offered arrival trace. Also retain the KServe controller digest, CRD versions, resolved base configs, rendered pod commands, endpoint-picker configuration, gateway implementation, dependency versions, scaling targets and relevant Kubernetes events. Secrets and prompt contents need not appear in shared reports; use identifiers and payload hashes where appropriate.

Declare the latency, quality, error and resource budgets before each run. Use the protocol's TTFT, first-answer latency, TPOT and goodput definitions. A chunk gap is not an exact token latency. Measure client-visible and engine-side results together, using synchronized clocks for cross-component traces. Preserve failed requests, unschedulable pods and failed runs. Randomize comparison order, repeat runs and report uncertainty and workload-specific results.

IDDecisionRequired path
K01Is gateway/platform overhead acceptable?Direct endpoint and managed service, same engine
K02Does prefix-aware routing improve useful cache reuse?At least two qualified replicas and a compatible endpoint picker
K03Does routing preserve latency under uneven work?Fixed replica pool, mixed workloads and controlled arrival process
K04Does P/D separation improve goodput within the same GPU budget?Both roles fit and transfer works for the exact model
K05Does scaling respond soon enough, including cold starts?Explicit metric source, actuator and observable replica targets
K06Does cancellation and draining release work predictably?Stream-capable client and engine/cache instrumentation
K07Can the service roll forward and back within its availability budget?Pinned old/new bundles and sufficient rollout capacity
K08Does master's SGLang template preserve the qualified engine behavior?Source/image provenance for the unreleased template path

The matrix gives local experiment identifiers, not KServe API fields or runnable manifests. Derive manifests from the selected commit's schemas and samples, then inspect their resolved output. The included renderer covers only custom-container Standard InferenceService.

K01 — Gateway and service overhead

Hypothesis. The managed request path adds acceptable latency and CPU cost while preserving the engine's response semantics. The budget comes from the application; there is no assumed universal overhead in milliseconds.

Control and change. Route an identical replay first to the qualified pod/service endpoint and then through the production-shaped gateway path to the same engine pod. Place the client consistently, hold TLS/authentication and connection reuse policy explicit, disable autoscaling and use one fixed replica. If comparing Standard InferenceService with LLMInferenceService, first verify the rendered commands, resources, cache and engine image are equivalent; then treat that wrapper comparison as a separate arm.

Procedure and measurements. Start at concurrency one with short output, then repeat the application's input/output mix below and near saturation. Cover streaming text, reasoning/tool deltas where supported, finish reasons, usage counts and long responses. Retain client TTFT/end-to-end distributions, engine request times, gateway CPU/memory, upstream errors, connection resets and timeout counts. Report the distribution difference and traced extra stages; do not subtract unrelated p99 samples and call the result per-request overhead.

Gate. Promote when quality and response semantics match the direct baseline and the added latency/resource cost stays inside the preregistered platform allowance. Any truncated stream, swallowed failure or response buffering that violates the streaming objective blocks promotion. This becomes the reference path for K02–K07.

K02 — Prefix locality versus load

Hypothesis. Sending related prompts to a replica with reusable state saves prefill work, but a heavily loaded cache owner can erase that benefit.

Control and change. Use at least two identical, independently qualified replicas. Compare a documented policy without prefix preference against a compatible policy with it, keeping the replica count and total GPUs fixed. Confirm the active configuration: stable 0.21.0's shipped router is 0.10.0 and uses its legacy default unless explicit/preserved configuration intervenes; the newer preset path requires router 0.11.0. Do not mix controller and router changes into a routing-only attribution. KEXP-COMPOSE21 KEXP-SCHEDULER21

Procedure and measurements. Replay token-identical shared prefixes, unrelated prefixes, a skewed hot-prefix workload and a workload whose hot prefix changes over time. Run cold and warmed conditions separately; disclose warmup requests and cost. Add eviction pressure and a replica restart. Record chosen endpoint, actual engine prefix reuse/prefill work where exposed, queue time, cache occupancy/eviction, TTFT, per-replica load and total-node goodput. Router estimates are not ground-truth cache hits. For hybrid models, retain recurrent-state reuse evidence too.

Gate. Accept the policy only if the declared workload mix gains goodput or latency within its resource budget, without starving cold or minority traffic. Negative-control prefixes must not receive another request's state or content. Apply the declared tenant/cache-key policy and test isolation with synthetic prompts. If no trustworthy cache counter is available, label the result as a routing/latency comparison and leave the cache mechanism unproven.

K03 — Load balancing and scheduler failure

Hypothesis. A policy that accounts for queued and active work improves tail latency when requests have unequal costs; an even request count alone does not establish an even GPU workload.

Control and change. Keep the replica pool fixed. Compare K02's selected policy with a documented alternative that emphasizes current load. Use the same model, cache state and arrival trace. Introduce mixtures of short and long prompts, short and long outputs, and a hot-prefix group competing with new prompts. Add vision traffic only after its direct path is qualified, and account for its preprocessing/token cost separately.

Procedure and measurements. Use an open-loop load generator for arrival-rate and overload experiments; the atlas's closed-loop SSE probe is insufficient to establish the offered-load curve. Measure queue/TTFT/end-to-end distributions by workload class, SLO-compliant goodput, timeouts, rejections, per-replica token work and scheduler CPU/decision latency. Keep failures in the offered-request denominator. In an isolated run, restart or interrupt the endpoint picker and observe the actual gateway response. The stable scheduler template declares failureMode: FailOpen; that declaration alone does not prove how the installed gateway behaves during failure. KEXP-EPPSERVICE21

Gate. Require the chosen policy to meet each declared workload-class latency target and overload behavior, with no persistent starvation. Document the observed fallback or fail-open behavior and its recovery time. An aggregate throughput win that breaches the short-request objective is not sufficient for that workload.

K04 — Prefill/decode separation and transfer economics

Hypothesis. Separating prompt processing from generation can reduce interference enough to pay for duplicated weights, state transfer, buffering and extra routing. The crossover depends on prompt/output lengths, load, cache reuse and the transport.

Control and change. Compare colocated serving with P/D at the same total GPU allocation and quality objective. First qualify each role's topology and the exact connector directly. For eight GPUs, a TP4 prefill plus TP4 decode arm is eligible only if each role independently fits and works. A model needing TP8 for each role requires sixteen GPUs under that assumption; mark it blocked on additional capacity rather than call an unequal-resource comparison an eight-GPU win.

Procedure and measurements. Begin with a model whose full state-transfer contract is established. Sweep the protocol's eligible context/output lengths, arrival load and prefill/decode allocation; retain both low-load and saturation behavior. Confirm actual transfer and decoding from transferred state. The stable presets conditionally add NIXL producer/consumer connectors and can leave transfer disabled when NIXL is unavailable, so successful pod health checks are insufficient. Record connector/transport identity, bytes and transfer time, prefill repeated at decode, queue time by role, TTFT, generation latency, goodput and peak memory. KEXP-DEC21 KEXP-PREFILL21

Repeat correctness checks through cache eviction, cancellation and a role restart. For hybrid models, explicitly include recurrent/conv state and speculative rollback where enabled. Treat independent Deployments/LeaderWorkerSets and master's optional DisaggregatedSet as separate orchestration arms after the transfer path passes; changing workload ownership does not by itself establish faster transfer.

Gate. Require a repeatable goodput or latency improvement at equal resources and quality, including the declared failure/recovery budget. Publish the crossover region and cases where colocated serving wins. Unknown state-transfer compatibility, decode recomputing the prompt unexpectedly, or missing transfer evidence leaves the advanced configuration unqualified.

K05 — Scaling, cold start and wake-up

Hypothesis. Demand-based scaling can save idle GPU time without missing the application latency objective if the metric arrives early enough and new replicas become useful before the queue exceeds its budget.

Control and change. Use fixed replicas as the reference, then one explicit direct-KEDA configuration at the same maximum GPU budget. Declare the metric, scrape/poll cadence, activation threshold, minimum/maximum replicas, cooldown, fallback and request admission policy. Use settings supported by the selected schemas. Stable 0.21.0 also offers WVA paths, but master rejects new WVA configuration and reports existing WVA scaling as unsupported; a stable-WVA-to-master upgrade is a separate migration case. KEXP-API21 KEXP-VALIDATIONMASTER KEXP-SCALINGMASTER

Procedure and measurements. Replay steps, bursts, ramps and idle periods. Separate image-cold, model-cache-cold and already-cached starts. Timestamp demand observation, desired-replica change, scheduling, image availability, model load, warmup, readiness, route inclusion and first successful request. Measure useful-capacity delay, queue area, errors/retries, TTFT during scale-up, wasted GPU-seconds and scale-down oscillation. If reporting a cost, supply the actual price basis; GPU-seconds alone are a resource measure.

Only add scale-to-zero after demonstrating a demand signal that survives zero engine pods. Test the first request, a burst during startup, metric loss and restoration, and the declared buffering/rejection behavior. For master's DisaggregatedSet arm, observe the actual role-scaler target and test zero-to-one-to-many transitions for both roles; the October 15 fix specifically concerns zero-replica scale-up. KEXP-DSKEDA KEXP-DSZERO

Gate. Promote only if scale-up and wake-up meet the declared request/availability budgets, missing metrics produce the chosen fallback, and no uncontrolled replica oscillation persists. A replica marked ready before successful warmup or a zero-pod state that cannot observe new demand fails the gate. On a fully occupied TP8 node, increasing desired replicas without additional schedulable GPUs is a capacity failure, not autoscaler throughput.

K06 — Cancellation, timeouts and draining

Hypothesis. Client cancellation propagates through the gateway/router to the engine quickly enough to bound wasted work and release sequence state, and shutdown handles admitted work predictably.

Control and change. Exercise the same qualified engine directly and through K01's managed path. Cancel at distinct stages: queued, mid-prefill, after first content and mid-decode. Repeat with cache hits, long contexts and P/D where eligible. Distinguish an explicit client disconnect from gateway timeout and pod termination; they need separate results.

Procedure and measurements. Correlate a synthetic request ID across client, gateway and engine. Observe engine work after disconnect, time until active/queued counts settle, KV/recurrent pool occupancy, memory plateau and subsequent-request correctness. Repeat cancellations to reveal accumulating state, then drain and terminate a replica under normal traffic. Record requests accepted during draining, completed/truncated streams, retries, duplicate responses and time to restore capacity. Retain response semantics for tool calls without executing returned tool content.

The atlas's SSE runner does not guarantee a strict wall-clock abort or expose engine-internal release time. Use a client that can deliberately close the stream and endpoint/engine instrumentation for this experiment; a client timeout exception alone does not prove cancellation reached the GPU. Runner boundaries

Gate. Require the preregistered cancellation/drain bounds, correct subsequent outputs and no accumulating request state or sustained memory growth across repetitions. A retry policy must not silently duplicate user-visible work. For hybrid/speculative paths, any state corruption blocks promotion even when latency improves.

K07 — Rolling update, migration and rollback

Hypothesis. A pinned update can reach useful new capacity and return to the old bundle within an explicit availability budget, while preserving stream semantics and the model/config identity of each response.

Control and change. Separate three cases: engine-image change under one controller, controller/router bundle change with the engine held fixed, and workload-backend migration. Retain old/new manifests, digests, CRDs, templates, routing config and model identity. Validate schema/conversion compatibility and rehearse recovery in a disposable environment before involving an existing service. A rollout requiring another TP8 replica needs another eight-GPU allocation; otherwise predeclare a service interruption or use a separately qualified topology with spare capacity.

Procedure and measurements. Send steady short traffic plus long streams during update and rollback. Include a replacement that never becomes ready, one that fails warmup, and an interruption of an engine worker. Measure unavailable time, successful/failed/truncated streams, revision mix, routing-convergence time, duplicate requests, queue growth and time to restore the previous service. Check that old scheduling/scaling resources do not continue controlling the wrong workload.

For DisaggregatedSet, distinguish rolling within an existing backend from moving onto or off it. The inspected controller warns that backend migration replaces old workloads without waiting for new readiness. Exercise that case with an explicit outage budget. Also cover zero surge and one/small replica counts, which motivated #6378; observe both roles and their desired/ready revisions. A controller-image rollback alone does not prove CRD and stored-object compatibility. KEXP-DSMASTER KEXP-DSROLLOUT

Gate. Require successful recovery within the declared budget, no silent truncated success, and an auditable returned-to-service bundle. Publish any planned downtime and spare-capacity requirement. An unready replacement that stalls forever, an unexpected WVA shutdown, or route/scaler resources targeting the wrong revision fails the gate.

K08 — Qualify the new SGLang template independently

Hypothesis. The inspected master template can preserve a directly qualified SGLang single-node deployment while adding the intended Kubernetes lifecycle and routing behavior.

Control and change. Compare that exact SGLang image through a custom-container Standard InferenceService and through master's dedicated runtime/template path. The source selects the SGLang template for single-node non-P/D services via spec.runtime; the bundled runtime defaults to SGLang 0.5.14. Explicitly pin the already-qualified current engine image rather than silently switching engine versions with the wrapper. This path is absent from the inspected 0.21.0 release. KEXP-COMPOSEMASTER KEXP-SGLANGMASTER KEXP-SGLANGRUNTIME

Procedure and measurements. Inspect resolved command, runtime image precedence, model mount, TP degree, shared memory, writable paths, probes and termination behavior. Run K01 and K06 first. Then test an endpoint-picker configuration only after identifying compatible SGLang metrics and endpoint metadata; if those cannot be established, record the routing-dependent arm as blocked with the missing contract. Compare outputs, load/warmup time, memory, protocol completeness and useful goodput to the custom-container control.

Gate. Promote only the behavior actually exercised: a passing single-node template test does not qualify multi-node, P/D transfer, hybrid-state migration or every llm-d cache plugin. Record engine-specific integration gaps explicitly and retain the simpler qualified service as the rollback reference.

Reporting a result

A result should state the exact comparison, workload, source/image identities, quality and SLO gates, capacity budget, failures and uncertainty. Include the rendered configuration and measurement artifacts needed to repeat it. Describe the decision as supported for that workload and topology; distinguish a source feature, a successful schema check, an upstream test claim and a locally reproduced serving result. Until these experiments run, their status remains proposed.

frameworks/sglang/EXPERIMENTS.md

SGLang experiments for one 8×B300 node

Planned experiments; no serving results. Start with the shared experiment roadmap, manifest template, benchmark protocol, hardware inventory and source review. These plans preserve the atlas's exact model revisions. A new recipe or successful import does not turn a compatibility probe into a validated profile.

Common controls and decision gates

Retain checkpoint, tokenizer, template and optional drafter revisions; image platform/digest or wheel hash; SGLang source identity; CUDA, driver, PyTorch, FlashInfer and communication versions; environment variables; rendered arguments; effective backends; node inventory; payload hashes; and raw responses. Confirm the desired source change is included. The observed wheel, development container and current main have different source abbreviations. GEXP-NIGHTLY-WHEELS, GEXP-NIGHTLY-TAGS.

Predeclare quality thresholds, allowed regression margins, p95 TTFT/TPOT budgets and failure policy. Match real prompts, visual payload limits, reasoning mode, sampling and stop conditions. Sweep 4K/16K/32K inputs first, then longer inputs only after qualification. Serialized input tokens, including template and visual tokens, plus the requested output budget must fit both the configured and model context limits. A 32K input plus output does not fit a 32K context cap; skip that cell or qualify a larger cap separately. Include concurrency 1/8/32 where capacity permits and load immediately below saturation. Randomize order, repeat independent runs and retain failures and uncertainty.

Use successful request goodput at the declared quality/latency targets as the common decision metric. Also retain p50/p95/p99 TTFT, generation and end-to-end latency, output tokens per total wall time, error rate, per-rank HBM peaks and CPU memory. Network chunk spacing is not token timing unless individual emission timestamps are available. Reject faster treatments that fail quality or reliability.

Real target prefill and natural draft acceptance are required for service/quality results. Fake KV/prefill, simulated acceptance, dummy weights and truncated synthetic models belong in separately labeled mechanical probes. Single-batch timings are diagnostics: SGLang documents that one batch does not represent steady-state serving. GEXP-BENCH.
